1. Four Things to Make Clear First

This is not about dates. Nothing in this section calculates the day, the year or the season of the rapture or the Second Coming. No one knows that day and hour (Matt. 24:36).
This is not a matter of salvation. Whether the rapture comes before, in the middle of, or after the Tribulation is a difference of interpretation that has long been discussed within historic orthodox faith. Which view you hold does not decide your salvation.
We respect every view. The faithful believers before us who held premillennialism, amillennialism or postmillennialism were all waiting for the same Lord. This section only explains why the pretribulation rapture is persuasive. It does not attack other views.
We keep pictures and proof apart. Stories such as those of Noah, Lot and Enoch, or the wedding customs, are pictures (types) that help us understand the Bible. We do not build doctrine on pictures alone. We read them together with the direct words of Scripture.
Next2. What Is the Rapture? — Starting with the Word
← Contents

2. What Is the Rapture? — Starting with the Word

The rapture is the event in which the Lord descends into the air: believers who have died in Christ are raised first, and believers who are still alive are changed in body and caught up together with them to meet the Lord.

16For the Lord himself shall descend from heaven with a shout, with the voice of the archangel, and with the trump of God: and the dead in Christ shall rise first: 17Then we which are alive and remain shall be caught up together with them in the clouds, to meet the Lord in the air: and so shall we ever be with the Lord.

1 Thessalonians 4:16-17 · KJV

The Greek word translated "caught up" is harpazō (ἁρπάζω). It means to snatch away suddenly and by force.1 Its meaning becomes even clearer when we see how the same word is used elsewhere in the New Testament.

  • The Spirit of the Lord caught away Philip (Acts 8:39) — moved him in an instant to another place
  • Paul was caught up to the third heaven (2 Cor. 12:2, 4) — taken up into heaven
  • The chief captain had his soldiers take him by force from among the crowd (Acts 23:10) — rescued by force from danger
  • Some are saved by pulling them out of the fire (Jude 1:23) — rescued just before destruction
  • The child born to the woman was caught up unto God and to His throne (Rev. 12:5) — out of the enemy's hand and into heaven

Around the fourth century the Bible was translated into Latin (the Vulgate), and it rendered these words in 1 Thess. 4:17 as rapiemur ("we shall be caught up"). The root is rapio ("to snatch"), and from it comes the English word "rapture." It is a theological term that simply names what this verse describes: being lifted up and carried away.

So the rapture is not people escaping on their own. It is a rescue: the Lord takes hold of His people in an instant and lifts them up. The word itself carries that picture.

51Behold, I shew you a mystery; We shall not all sleep, but we shall all be changed, 52In a moment, in the twinkling of an eye, at the last trump: for the trumpet shall sound, and the dead shall be raised incorruptible, and we shall be changed.

1 Corinthians 15:51-52 · KJV

Paul calls this a "mystery," that is, a new revelation that was not made known in the Old Testament. It is the promise that there will be a generation who are changed in an instant without passing through death.

Previous1. Four Things to Make Clear FirstNext3. A Map of the Views — the Timing of the Rapture and the Millennium
← Contents

3. A Map of the Views — the Timing of the Rapture and the Millennium

Most Bible-believing scholars agree that there will be a rapture. Where they differ is when. The reference point is Daniel's seventieth week, the seven-year Tribulation that comes just before the Lord returns to the earth.2

ViewTiming of the raptureRepresentative works
Pretribulation raptureBefore the seven-year Tribulation beginsWalvoord, Pentecost, Blaising
Midtribulation raptureAt the midpoint of the seven-year TribulationArcher
Pre-wrath raptureBefore God's wrath is poured out in the latter halfHultberg
Posttribulation raptureWhen the Tribulation ends, at the same time as the coming to the earthLadd, Gundry, Moo

Views also differ on how to read the "thousand years" of Revelation 20. Premillennialism holds that the Lord comes again before the millennial kingdom. It has two forms, historic premillennialism and dispensational premillennialism. Amillennialism reads the "thousand years" as a symbol of the church age, from the Ascension to the Second Coming. Postmillennialism holds that the Lord comes after the gospel has spread widely throughout the world. All of these are interpretations that faithful believers have held throughout church history.

Dispensationalism is a way of reading the Bible that was organized into a system in the nineteenth century. It reads the history of the Bible in several eras (dispensations), distinguishes Israel from the church, and explains the last days in this order: pretribulation rapture → seven-year Tribulation → Second Coming → Millennium. Dispensationalism is one of the systems of interpretation that have long been discussed within the orthodox church. It is not a heresy that denies the faith confessed in the Apostles' Creed. It is true that some have borrowed its name to set dates, to make people follow a particular leader, or to make them give away their property. Such deviations have nothing to do with this interpretation.

All four views are differences of interpretation within historic orthodox faith. The timing of the rapture is not a condition of salvation.3
Previous2. What Is the Rapture? — Starting with the WordNext4. Ten Lines of Biblical Evidence for the Pretribulation Rapture
← Contents

4. Ten Lines of Biblical Evidence for the Pretribulation Rapture

① He may come at any time — imminence

And to wait for his Son from heaven, whom he raised from the dead, even Jesus, which delivered us from the wrath to come.

1 Thessalonians 1:10 · KJV

For our conversation is in heaven; from whence also we look for the Saviour, the Lord Jesus Christ:

Philippians 3:20 · KJV

Grudge not one against another, brethren, lest ye be condemned: behold, the judge standeth before the door.

James 5:9 · KJV

Therefore be ye also ready: for in such an hour as ye think not the Son of man cometh.

Matthew 24:44 · KJV

The believers of the New Testament did not say that the Lord would come only after certain signs had all been fulfilled. They waited for a Lord who might come that very day. This is called imminence. If the rapture happened at the end of the seven-year Tribulation, people could count the days from the day the Tribulation began, that is, from the day the covenant of Daniel 9:27 is confirmed. A rapture that waits for no sign best keeps the words "in such an hour as ye think not" and "of that day and hour knoweth no man."4

② He delivers us from wrath

For the great day of his wrath is come; and who shall be able to stand?

Revelation 6:17 · KJV

For God hath not appointed us to wrath, but to obtain salvation by our Lord Jesus Christ,

1 Thessalonians 5:9 · KJV

Much more then, being now justified by his blood, we shall be saved from wrath through him.

Romans 5:9 · KJV

In Revelation, already at the opening of the sixth seal the people of the earth cry out, "the great day of his wrath is come" (Rev. 6:16–17), and with the seven last plagues "is filled up the wrath of God" (Rev. 15:1). If the Tribulation is thus a time when God's wrath comes upon the earth, the promise that believers are not appointed to that wrath fits best with being taken out of that period.

③ He keeps us from the "hour" of trial

Because thou hast kept the word of my patience, I also will keep thee from the hour of temptation, which shall come upon all the world, to try them that dwell upon the earth.

Revelation 3:10 · KJV

"Keep thee from" translates the Greek tēreō ek (τηρέω ἐκ), "to keep out of." The Lord does not say He will protect the church in the middle of the trial. He says He will keep it from the very "hour" (hōra) of trial that will come upon the whole world.5 The objections to this verse and our answers are covered in detail in Objection 5 below.

④ He rescues first, then judges — Noah, Lot and Enoch

28Likewise also as it was in the days of Lot; they did eat, they drank, they bought, they sold, they planted, they builded; 29But the same day that Lot went out of Sodom it rained fire and brimstone from heaven, and destroyed them all. 30Even thus shall it be in the day when the Son of man is revealed.

Luke 17:28-30 · KJV

Haste thee, escape thither; for I cannot do anything till thou be come thither. Therefore the name of the city was called Zoar.

Genesis 19:22 · KJV

The Lord knoweth how to deliver the godly out of temptations, and to reserve the unjust unto the day of judgment to be punished:

2 Peter 2:9 · KJV

The Lord compared the day of the Son of man to the days of Noah and the days of Lot. Both stories follow the same order. God's people are first moved to a safe place, and then judgment falls. The angel at Sodom said, "I cannot do anything till thou be come thither." The judgment was already decided, but it could not begin until the righteous man had gone out.

By faith Enoch was translated that he should not see death; and was not found, because God had translated him: for before his translation he had this testimony, that he pleased God.

Hebrews 11:5 · KJV

Long before Noah went into the ark, Enoch was taken to God without seeing death. Many interpreters have read Enoch, taken away before the Flood, as a picture of the church caught up in the rapture, and Noah, preserved through the Flood, as a picture of the remnant kept through the Tribulation. This is a type, so it is not proof in itself. But it points in the same direction as the direct words of Scripture above.

⑤ "Escape all these things"

Watch ye therefore, and pray always, that ye may be accounted worthy to escape all these things that shall come to pass, and to stand before the Son of man.

Luke 21:36 · KJV

The Greek word translated "escape" is ekpheugō (ἐκφεύγω), which means to get out of, to get away from.6 The Lord did not only tell us to endure all these things. He told us to pray that we may "escape" them, and He ended with "to stand before the Son of man." This is not a picture of passing through the troubles on earth. It is a picture of being brought before the Lord.

⑥ The Bridegroom comes at midnight — the parable of the ten virgins

And at midnight there was a cry made, Behold, the bridegroom cometh; go ye out to meet him.

Matthew 25:6 · KJV

And while they went to buy, the bridegroom came; and they that were ready went in with him to the marriage: and the door was shut.

Matthew 25:10 · KJV

Watch therefore, for ye know neither the day nor the hour wherein the Son of man cometh.

Matthew 25:13 · KJV
  • The bridegroom comes without warning, at midnight, when the wait has grown long and everyone has fallen asleep.
  • Those who are ready go in with him to the marriage. The bridegroom comes in order to take the bride.
  • Then the door is shut. Some people are left outside.
  • The point of the parable is not calculation but readiness: "Watch therefore."

⑦ The Bridegroom takes the bride to the Father's house — the Jewish wedding custom

The New Testament calls the church the bride of Christ (2 Cor. 11:2; Eph. 5:25–27; Rev. 19:7). The order of the Jewish wedding custom, as it is known from the time of Jesus, is remarkably like the way the Lord deals with His church.7

Wedding customChrist and the church
① Betrothal — the bridegroom pays a price and they share a cup of the covenant. From this point they are legally husband and wife.Believers "bought with a price" (1 Cor. 6:20); "This cup is the new testament in my blood" (Luke 22:20)
② The bridegroom goes back to his father's house and prepares a place to live with his bride."I go to prepare a place for you" (John 14:2)
③ The father decides the day the bridegroom goes to fetch her.That day and hour only the Father knows (Matt. 24:36)
④ The bride does not know when he will come, so she stays ready and pure as she waits."Every man that hath this hope in him purifieth himself" (1 John 3:3)
⑤ The bridegroom comes suddenly at night, with a shout and the sound of a trumpet."At midnight there was a cry made" (Matt. 25:6); "with a shout ... and with the trump of God" (1 Thess. 4:16)
⑥ The bridegroom does not stay at the bride's house. He takes the bride away with him."I will come again, and receive you unto myself" (John 14:3); "to meet the Lord in the air" (1 Thess. 4:17)
⑦ The wedding feast is held for seven days at the father's house."Fulfil her week" (Gen. 29:27); "the seven days of the feast" (Judg. 14:12); "the marriage of the Lamb" (Rev. 19:7)
⑧ After the feast, the bridegroom and the bride appear together."The armies which were in heaven followed him" (Rev. 19:14); "then shall ye also appear with him in glory" (Col. 3:4)

Seen through this picture, Christ the Bridegroom first comes and takes His bride, the church, to the Father's house (the rapture). While the seven-year Tribulation passes on earth, the marriage supper of the Lamb is held in heaven. After that, He appears in glory together with His bride (the Second Coming).

This beginning of miracles did Jesus in Cana of Galilee, and manifested forth his glory; and his disciples believed on him.

John 2:11 · KJV

It is also meaningful that Jesus' first sign took place at a wedding feast on "the third day." The ruler of the feast said, "thou hast kept the good wine until now" (John 2:1, 10). The Lord's ministry began at a wedding feast, and it will be completed at the marriage supper of the Lamb. The best has been kept for last.

Many interpreters and preachers have described this custom on the basis of ancient Jewish writings, and the details may have differed by region and period. It is a beautiful picture for understanding the Bible, but it is not proof of doctrine in itself. Please read it together with the direct words of Scripture above.

⑧ The coming in the air and the coming to the earth are different scenes

And his feet shall stand in that day upon the mount of Olives, which is before Jerusalem on the east, and the mount of Olives shall cleave in the midst thereof toward the east and toward the west, and there shall be a very great valley; and half of the mountain shall remove toward the north, and half of it toward the south.

Zechariah 14:4 · KJV

11And I saw heaven opened, and behold a white horse; and he that sat upon him was called Faithful and True, and in righteousness he doth judge and make war. 14And the armies which were in heaven followed him upon white horses, clothed in fine linen, white and clean.

Revelation 19:11,14 · KJV

When Christ, who is our life, shall appear, then shall ye also appear with him in glory.

Colossians 3:4 · KJV
Rapture (coming in the air)Coming to the earth
WhereBelievers meet the Lord in the air (1 Thess. 4:17)His feet stand on the Mount of Olives (Zech. 14:4)
With whomHe comes for His saints (John 14:3)He comes with His saints (Col. 3:4; Rev. 19:14)
DirectionBelievers go from earth to heavenThe Lord and His saints come from heaven to earth
Mood"Comfort one another with these words" (1 Thess. 4:18)All the tribes of the earth mourn (Matt. 24:30)
SignsNo sign — at any time (Matt. 24:44)After the sun and moon are darkened (Matt. 24:29–30)
PurposeTo take His bride (John 14:3)To judge and to reign (Rev. 19:11–15)

If these are merged into one event, the texts collide. Read as two stages with the Tribulation between them, each text finds its place.

⑨ The flow of Revelation — the word "church" disappears

In Revelation 1–3 the word "church" (ekklēsia) appears nineteen times. But in chapters 4–18, which describe the Tribulation on earth, the church on earth is not mentioned even once. Then the word appears again in 22:16.8

After this I looked, and, behold, a door was opened in heaven: and the first voice which I heard was as it were of a trumpet talking with me; which said, Come up hither, and I will shew thee things which must be hereafter.

Revelation 4:1 · KJV

Chapter 4 begins with the call "Come up hither." And already around the throne in heaven sit twenty-four elders, clothed in white and wearing crowns of gold (Rev. 4:4). Many interpreters read them as representatives of the church, already taken up to heaven and rewarded.

⑩ When the restrainer is taken out of the way

7For the mystery of iniquity doth already work: only he who now letteth will let, until he be taken out of the way. 8And then shall that Wicked be revealed, whom the Lord shall consume with the spirit of his mouth, and shall destroy with the brightness of his coming:

2 Thessalonians 2:7-8 · KJV

Before the man of sin is revealed, the one who now restrains him must first be taken out of the way. The pretribulation view understands this restrainer as the work of the Holy Spirit, who dwells in the church and holds back the lawlessness of the world. It reads that this restraint is lifted when the church is caught up.9

Previous3. A Map of the Views — the Timing of the Rapture and the MillenniumNext5. Israel and the Church — Whom Is the Final Week For?
← Contents

5. Israel and the Church — Whom Is the Final Week For?

Replacement theology is the view that the church has replaced Israel. Because Israel rejected Christ, it says, the promises God gave to Israel in the Old Testament have now all passed spiritually to the church, and God's plan for Israel as a people is finished. It is also a view that has been widely held for a long time in church history.

I say then, Hath God cast away his people? God forbid. For I also am an Israelite, of the seed of Abraham, of the tribe of Benjamin.

Romans 11:1 · KJV

25For I would not, brethren, that ye should be ignorant of this mystery, lest ye should be wise in your own conceits; that blindness in part is happened to Israel, until the fulness of the Gentiles be come in. 26And so all Israel shall be saved: as it is written, There shall come out of Sion the Deliverer, and shall turn away ungodliness from Jacob:

Romans 11:25-26 · KJV

For the gifts and calling of God are without repentance.

Romans 11:29 · KJV

Seventy weeks are determined upon thy people and upon thy holy city, to finish the transgression, and to make an end of sins, and to make reconciliation for iniquity, and to bring in everlasting righteousness, and to seal up the vision and prophecy, and to anoint the most Holy.

Daniel 9:24 · KJV

Alas! for that day is great, so that none is like it: it is even the time of Jacob’s trouble, but he shall be saved out of it.

Jeremiah 30:7 · KJV

Paul says that God has not cast away His people, and that His calling is "without repentance." Daniel's seventy weeks are determined "upon thy people and upon thy holy city," and Jeremiah calls the final trouble "the time of Jacob’s trouble." So the pretribulation view does not see the seven-year Tribulation as a time for refining the church. It sees it as the time when God calls Israel back to Himself and judges the world. When the church and Israel are read as distinct, the order in which the church, the bride, is taken away beforehand follows naturally. On the other hand, if the church has completely replaced Israel, then the believers who go through the Tribulation must also be the church, and the reading leans toward placing the rapture after the Tribulation.

For he is our peace, who hath made both one, and hath broken down the middle wall of partition between us;

Ephesians 2:14 · KJV
This distinction does not mean that there are two ways of salvation. Jews and Gentiles alike are saved only in Christ, only by faith. This article also neither supports nor criticizes the politics or wars of any nation today, and it does not condemn brothers and sisters who hold replacement theology.
Previous4. Ten Lines of Biblical Evidence for the Pretribulation RaptureNext6. Traces in Church History
← Contents

6. Traces in Church History

The pretribulation rapture was organized into a system in the nineteenth century. But the seeds of the idea can be seen much earlier.

  • The early church's eager waiting — The Didache, a church document from the early second century, urges believers in chapter 16 to keep watch, because they do not know the hour when the Lord is coming. The early church did not push the Lord's coming off into the distant future.10
  • Hippolytus (early third century) — He saw Daniel's final "week" as a period that remains at the end of the world, after the gospel has been preached to the whole world. He did not teach a rapture. But this is a trace showing that the reading which sets the final week apart and places it in the future existed from a very early time.11
  • An old sermon handed down under the name of Ephraem (4th–7th century) — It contains a sentence to the effect that all the saints and the elect are gathered and taken to the Lord before the coming tribulation, so that they do not see the confusion that will overwhelm the world. Scholars discuss how this sentence should be read, so we present it as a "source under discussion."12
  • After the Reformation — Preachers and commentators who read the Bible literally and took prophecy seriously gradually set forth the idea of distinguishing the rapture from the Second Coming. A 1788 work by the American Baptist minister Morgan Edwards is a leading example. The idea was then organized into a system in the nineteenth century.13

What matters is not so much when an interpretation was organized into a system, but whether it comes from the biblical text. The Trinity and justification by faith were also formally stated at particular times, but their roots are in Scripture.

Previous5. Israel and the Church — Whom Is the Final Week For?Next7. Seventeen Common Objections and Answers
← Contents

7. Seventeen Common Objections and Answers

In fairness, we set the questions raised by other views side by side with the answers of the pretribulation view. The brothers and sisters who ask these questions are waiting for the same Lord. Being awake on that day matters more than who turns out to be right.

Q1. In Matthew 24:31 the elect are gathered "after the tribulation of those days." Doesn't that put the rapture after the Tribulation?

And he shall send his angels with a great sound of a trumpet, and they shall gather together his elect from the four winds, from one end of heaven to the other.

Matthew 24:31 · KJV

The Olivet Discourse is spoken first of all with "them which be in Judaea" (24:16) and "the sabbath day" (24:20) in view. Here the ones who gather the elect from the four winds are the angels. It is the scene in which God fulfills His promise to gather scattered Israel with the sound of a great trumpet (Isa. 27:13; Deut. 30:4). The rapture is different. The Lord Himself comes down, and believers meet Him in the air (1 Thess. 4:16–17). The Second Coming is a day that all the tribes of the earth will see and mourn (Matt. 24:30), but the rapture happens in the twinkling of an eye and brings "comfort" to believers (1 Thess. 4:18). These "elect" are most naturally read as those who come to faith during the Tribulation after the rapture, together with the remnant of Israel whom God gathers again.14

Q2. Isn't "the last trump" (1 Cor. 15:52) the seventh trumpet of Revelation (Rev. 11:15)? Both passages speak of a "mystery."
  • They were written at different times. First Corinthians was written about forty years before Revelation. The Corinthian believers could not have known about a seventh trumpet that had not yet been written down. So Paul's "last trump" is most naturally read as something he had already taught them: "the trump of God" that gathers the saints (1 Thess. 4:16).
  • Who blows it, and what follows, are different. The trumpet of the rapture is "the trump of God," and it ends with "Wherefore comfort one another with these words" (1 Thess. 4:18). The seven trumpets of Revelation are trumpets of judgment blown by angels, and even after the seventh trumpet come "the third woe" (Rev. 11:14) and the seven bowl judgments.
  • "Mystery" is not the name of a single event. The New Testament word "mystery" (mystērion) appears more than twenty times. It is used for many different things: the blindness of Israel (Rom. 11:25), the church (Eph. 3:3–6), Christ and the church (Eph. 5:32), the seven stars (Rev. 1:20) and Babylon the great (Rev. 17:5). What is more, the mystery in Rev. 10:7 was already known, "as he hath declared to his servants the prophets," while the mystery Paul speaks of is one "which in other ages was not made known unto the sons of men" (Eph. 3:5).
  • If "last" means last in a sequence, there is also a trumpet after the seventh: the "great sound of a trumpet" at the Second Coming (Matt. 24:31). The single word "last" cannot tie the two trumpets together as one.
Q3. Doesn't 2 Thessalonians 2:3 say that the falling away and the man of sin must come "first" before that day comes?

Let no man deceive you by any means: for that day shall not come, except there come a falling away first, and that man of sin be revealed, the son of perdition;

2 Thessalonians 2:3 · KJV

The "day" Paul speaks of is not the rapture but the Day of the Lord, the day of judgment. The Thessalonian believers had been shaken by word that this day had already come (2 Thess. 2:2). Paul corrects them: that day has not yet come, and when it comes, the falling away and the revealing of the man of sin will go with it. He also says that the man of sin is revealed only after "he who now letteth" is taken out of the way (2:6–8).

That ye be not soon shaken in mind, or be troubled, neither by spirit, nor by word, nor by letter as from us, as that the day of Christ is at hand.

2 Thessalonians 2:2 · KJV

It is also worth noticing why they were shaken. If they had been taught that they would be caught up at the end of the Tribulation, the news that the Day of the Lord had come would actually have been welcome news. The natural explanation is that they were afraid because they had been taught the hope of being caught up before that day, and thought they had missed that gathering.15

Q4. Aren't the people in white robes who "came out of great tribulation" (Rev. 7:14) the church?

And I said unto him, Sir, thou knowest. And he said to me, These are they which came out of great tribulation, and have washed their robes, and made them white in the blood of the Lamb.

Revelation 7:14 · KJV

In the original, "came out" is in the present tense: "those who are coming out." It pictures people who turn to the Lord and are martyred throughout the Tribulation, arriving in heaven one after another. The church is already before the throne in chapter 4, as the twenty-four elders clothed in white and wearing crowns of gold (Rev. 4:4). That is why the elder asks John, "What are these which are arrayed in white robes? and whence came they?" (7:13). If they were the church, there would be no reason to ask. This multitude is evidence of grace: even after the rapture, God does not shut the door of salvation.

Q5. Doesn't "keep" (tēreō ek) in Rev. 3:10 mean "to preserve inside," as in John 17:15?

And now I am no more in the world, but these are in the world, and I come to thee. Holy Father, keep through thine own name those whom thou hast given me, that they may be one, as we are.

John 17:11 · KJV

I pray not that thou shouldest take them out of the world, but that thou shouldest keep them from the evil.

John 17:15 · KJV

John 17 uses two different kinds of "keeping." In verse 11, "keep through thine own name" is tēreō en (to keep within). In verse 15, "keep them from the evil" is tēreō ek (to keep out of). Verse 15 is also a prayer that the disciples be kept outside the grip of the evil one, not a prayer that they hold out while inside it. Rev. 3:10 uses ek, and what follows is not simply the trial but the hour (hōra) of trial, that is, a period of time.

Also, that hour is the hour "to try them that dwell upon the earth." In Revelation, "them that dwell upon the earth" is a name for those who oppose God (6:10; 13:8; 17:8). If the verse meant "preserved inside," it would not fit the passages that say many believers will be martyred during the Tribulation (6:9–11; 13:7). And if it meant only that God keeps their souls, that is already promised to every believer (John 10:28), so there would be no reason to promise it as a special reward to the church in Philadelphia alone.

To be fair, some scholars read ek as "coming out from within." Even read that way, the word alone does not decide whether that coming out happens before the hour of trial or at its end. So it must be read together with the context above.5

Q6. Doesn't the wrath begin only with the sixth seal, at the end of the Tribulation? Until then, aren't believers protected in the middle of it, as Israel was in Goshen?

The seals in Revelation are opened by the Lamb Himself, starting with the first one (Rev. 6:1). By the fourth seal, a fourth of the earth has already been killed (6:8). This is not human persecution; it is judgment that comes from the Lamb's hand. So when the kings of the earth cry out at the sixth seal, "the great day of his wrath is come" (6:17), it reads not as the moment the wrath begins, but as the moment they recognize the wrath that has already come. Examples of God keeping His people in the midst of trouble, such as Goshen (Exod. 8:22), the lions' den and the fiery furnace, are precious. But Rev. 7:3 and 9:4 say clearly that the servants who are sealed are from the twelve tribes of Israel (7:4–8). The pretribulation view says just as clearly that there will be believers in the Tribulation. They are the believers who turn to the Lord after the rapture.

Q7. Jesus said, "when ye shall see all these things, know that it is near" (Matt. 24:33), and "those days shall be shortened" (24:22). So even if the rapture is at the end of the Tribulation, no one could count the days, could they?

"Shall be shortened" does not mean that God keeps the day hidden. It means that He sets a limit to that period and cuts it off. In fact, Scripture gives exact numbers: 1,260 days and forty-two months (Rev. 11:2–3; 12:6; 13:5), and 1,290 days (Dan. 12:11). Counting from the day the abomination of desolation stands in the holy place (Matt. 24:15), the day can be worked out. Yet the Lord said, "in such an hour as ye think not the Son of man cometh" (Matt. 24:44), and "ye know neither the day nor the hour" (25:13). "Know that it is near" was spoken to the Tribulation saints who await the Second Coming. To the church He gave the imminent hope that He may come even today.

Q8. There is only one Second Coming. Isn't it unbiblical to say that He comes twice, once to the air and once to the earth? I've heard that "meet" is a word for going out to welcome a king and then coming back down to the earth with him.

The Second Coming is one event that takes place in two stages. The First Coming, too, held His birth, ministry, cross and resurrection within a single "coming." In the synagogue at Nazareth, Jesus read from Isa. 61:2, stopped at "the acceptable year of the Lord," and left "the day of vengeance of our God" for later (Luke 4:19). The Second Coming likewise has a scene in the air, when He comes for His saints (1 Thess. 4:17), and a scene on earth, when He comes with His saints (Zech. 14:5; Jude 1:14; Col. 3:4).

It is true that the Greek word translated "meet," apantēsis, was used in the ancient world for going out of a city to welcome an important official. But there is also research showing that the word itself does not decide which way people go after the meeting.16 Jesus said He would prepare a place "In my Father’s house" and "receive you unto myself; that where I am, there ye may be also" (John 14:2–3). The saints are in the Father's house, and then they come down following the Lord, clothed in white (Rev. 19:14).

Q9. In the Flood, those who were taken were the ones destroyed, and Noah was left. So isn't being taken a curse and being left a blessing?

39And knew not until the flood came, and took them all away; so shall also the coming of the Son of man be. 40Then shall two be in the field; the one shall be taken, and the other left.

Matthew 24:39-40 · KJV

In Matt. 24:40, "taken" is the Greek paralambanō, the very word Jesus used in "receive you unto myself" (John 14:3). But the word for the flood that "took them all away" (24:39) is an entirely different word, airō.17 Being taken is the Lord receiving His own; being left is judgment.

The point of the Lord's comparison with the days of Noah was ordinary daily life: people were "eating and drinking, marrying and giving in marriage" (Matt. 24:38). At the end of the Tribulation, a fourth of the earth (Rev. 6:8) and then a third of men (9:15) would already have died. Could the world still be marrying and giving in marriage as usual then? A picture of something sudden breaking into everyday life fits better before the Tribulation.

Q10. Noah was preserved through the Flood. Isn't the church, too, preserved through the Tribulation?

If Noah is a picture, so is Lot, and Lot's case is even clearer. The angel said, "I cannot do anything till thou be come thither" (Gen. 19:22), and "the same day that Lot went out of Sodom it rained fire and brimstone" (Luke 17:29). There is also Enoch, taken away before the Flood (Heb. 11:5). What these types show is not the length of the gap but the order: the righteous must go out first, and then judgment begins. Noah can be read as a picture of the remnant kept through the Tribulation, that is, those who turn to the Lord after the rapture.

Q11. "The first resurrection" in Rev. 20:5 comes after the Tribulation. Doesn't that mean there is no resurrection (rapture) before it?

And I saw thrones, and they sat upon them, and judgment was given unto them: and I saw the souls of them that were beheaded for the witness of Jesus, and for the word of God, and which had not worshipped the beast, neither his image, neither had received his mark upon their foreheads, or in their hands; and they lived and reigned with Christ a thousand years.

Revelation 20:4 · KJV

But every man in his own order: Christ the firstfruits; afterward they that are Christ’s at his coming.

1 Corinthians 15:23 · KJV

"The first resurrection" refers not to a single date but to a kind of resurrection: "the resurrection of life" (John 5:29), "the resurrection of the just" (Luke 14:14). Scripture says this resurrection happens to "every man in his own order": Christ the firstfruits, then those who are Christ's at His coming, and then the martyrs of the Tribulation, who did not worship the beast and were beheaded (Rev. 20:4). Rev. 20:4–5 is the scene in which the first resurrection is completed. It does not say that there was no resurrection before it. The proof is that the resurrection of Christ, the firstfruits of the first resurrection, already took place two thousand years ago.18

Q12. Don't believers go through the Tribulation and then get caught up at the end, together with the Second Coming?

Scripture distinguishes the two scenes (see the comparison table in ⑧ above). In Revelation, too, after "Come up hither" in chapter 4, the church does not appear on earth all the way through chapter 18. Then in chapter 19 the bride, made ready in heaven, comes down with the Lord (Rev. 19:7–14). If the rapture is at the end of the Tribulation, three questions remain for us to think about together.

  • The day no one knows. Counting 1,260 days (Rev. 12:6) or 1,290 days (Dan. 12:11) from the abomination of desolation (Matt. 24:15) would give the day. Yet the Lord said, "ye know neither the day nor the hour" (Matt. 25:13).
  • Those who enter the Millennium. If at the end of the Tribulation all believers are caught up and given glorified bodies, and the wicked are judged, it is hard to explain where certain people come from: those who enter the Millennium in mortal bodies and have children (Isa. 65:20), and the nations who will be deceived again after the thousand years (Rev. 20:7–8).
  • The saints who come with Him. The saints who follow the Lord from heaven at the Second Coming, clothed in fine linen (Rev. 19:8, 14), must already be in heaven before that (Col. 3:4; Zech. 14:5).

There shall be no more thence an infant of days, nor an old man that hath not filled his days: for the child shall die an hundred years old; but the sinner being an hundred years old shall be accursed.

Isaiah 65:20 · KJV

And to her was granted that she should be arrayed in fine linen, clean and white: for the fine linen is the righteousness of saints.

Revelation 19:8 · KJV
Q13. I've heard that the pretribulation rapture was invented by the sixteenth-century Jesuits (Catholic) to turn aside the interpretation that identified them with the harlot of Revelation. Is that true?

Let us first set out the historical facts. What the Jesuit theologian Ribera taught in the late sixteenth century was the interpretation that the Antichrist is one person who will come in the distant future, that is, futurism. It has nothing to do with the timing of the rapture, and most of those who believe in a posttribulation rapture hold this futurism too. The claim that "the Jesuit priest Lacunza wrote at the pope's command" is also untrue. In fact, the book on the Second Coming by Lacunza (1731–1801) was placed on the Catholic Church's Index of Forbidden Books in September 1824. A book written at the Vatican's command would hardly have been banned.19 As for the claim that he was a "secret Rosicrucian agent," no historical source has been found to support it. Moreover, official Catholic teaching does not accept the idea of an earthly millennial kingdom at all, so it would have had no reason to invent a rapture doctrine.20

The man who translated Lacunza's book into English (1827) was Edward Irving. That is why people often draw the line "Lacunza → Irving → Darby." But Irving himself did not teach a pretribulation rapture. And there are both claims that Darby borrowed the idea from Irving's circle and studies that refute those claims.21 Above all, the standard of truth is not "Where did it come from?" but "What does the Bible say?" (Acts 17:11).

Q14. Isn't this a doctrine that came from nineteenth-century dreams and visions? I've heard that the word "rapture" did not even exist until the late eighteenth century.

The basis is not dreams but the Word: "Jesus, which delivered us from the wrath to come" (1 Thess. 1:10); "God hath not appointed us to wrath, but to obtain salvation" (1 Thess. 5:9); "I also will keep thee from the hour of temptation" (Rev. 3:10); "I will come again, and receive you unto myself" (John 14:3). The claim that it began with the vision of a woman in Scotland in 1830 is also judged by scholars to have weak support.22

The word "rapture" comes from the fourth-century Latin Bible, which rendered 1 Thess. 4:17 with rapiemur. So its root has been in the Bible for more than 1,600 years. If we judged by when a word was first used, even the word "Trinity" was not used before the late second century.23 The standard is not the age of a word but the Word of God.

Q15. If it was organized into a system in the nineteenth century, isn't it a late idea, and therefore a wrong one?

It is true that it was organized into a system in the nineteenth century. But the text "caught up" (1 Thess. 4:17) and the teaching to stay awake at all times belonged to the church from the beginning (Section 6). Being organized late does not make an idea wrong.

For I will take you from among the heathen, and gather you out of all countries, and will bring you into your own land.

Ezekiel 36:24 · KJV

The restoration of Israel is a good example. Ezekiel 36–37 and Isaiah 11:11–12 say that the scattered people will be gathered back to their land. For most of church history, the mainstream interpretation explained these words only as figures of the church, and only a minority believed them literally. Nineteenth-century interpreters who read prophecy literally expected the scattered people to actually return to the land. When Israel became a nation again in 1948, it became clear that their reading had been faithful to the text. Yet the whole people have not yet turned to the Lord. In Ezekiel 37, too, the bones come together first, and the breath enters them later (Ezek. 37:7–10). This is not a judgment about today's international politics or conflicts. It is about the way we read the Word.

An old majority interpretation is not always right. The standard is always Scripture (Acts 17:11).

Q16. Isn't the pretribulation rapture a dangerous doctrine that leaves believers unprepared? Isn't it a faith that just wants to avoid suffering?

Those who are caught up are not the people who got the timing right. They are the people who are in Christ (1 Thess. 4:16–17). The timing is not a condition of salvation. Christians suffer hardship and persecution even now (John 16:33). What the pretribulation rapture says is not that there will be no suffering. It says that God will rescue His bride from the final period when His wrath is poured out (Rev. 6:16–17).

And every man that hath this hope in him purifieth himself, even as he is pure.

1 John 3:3 · KJV

The imminent hope that He may come even today actually keeps us awake now. It is rather the thought "there are still seven years left" that comes close to the danger of the servant who said, "My lord delayeth his coming" (Matt. 24:48). Scripture tells us to hold this hope firmly (Titus 2:13; Phil. 3:20; 1 Thess. 1:10). This hope is not running away. It is the strength to live a holy life today. Just as the apostles walked the road of martyrdom, the church has suffered for two thousand years, and that calling is the same whatever the timing.

Blessed is that servant, whom his lord when he cometh shall find so doing.

Matthew 24:46 · KJV

The command not to receive the mark of the beast is the same for everyone, whatever the timing. We also firmly oppose the real dangers: setting dates (Matt. 24:36), teaching that a person must gain some special insight to be saved, and making money from the end times.

Q17. But if the pretribulation rapture is wrong, won't believers be caught unprepared and deceived?

No one will be deceived without knowing it at that time. The Antichrist will sit in the temple and claim to be God (2 Thess. 2:4), make war with the saints (Rev. 13:7), and keep anyone from buying or selling who does not receive a mark on the right hand or the forehead (Rev. 13:16–17). This is open persecution of those who believe in Jesus, so anyone will recognize it, and Scripture has already clearly warned that the mark must not be received (Rev. 14:9–11). If, without a rapture, a covenant for one week is confirmed (Dan. 9:27), the falling away takes place (2 Thess. 2:3), the abomination of desolation stands in the holy place (Matt. 24:15) and the judgments of Revelation begin, we will know right away: "So it was after the Tribulation." Both the pretribulation and the posttribulation views believe this order, so knowing it then will not be too late, and nothing will be lost. Whatever view we hold, if we come to stand in that place, there is only one thing to do: hold fast to Christ to the end (see Section 8, next).

Previous6. Traces in Church HistoryNext8. What If a Figure Like the Antichrist Appears Without a Rapture?
← Contents

8. What If a Figure Like the Antichrist Appears Without a Rapture?

"I believed in a pretribulation rapture. What if someone like the Antichrist appears and the rapture has not happened? What if we have already entered the Tribulation?" This is a question we must answer honestly. This section is not meant to cause fear. It is a guide, set out in advance from the Word, to help us discern and respond without being shaken when we hear such news.

First: "Like the Antichrist" is not the same as "the Antichrist"

Little children, it is the last time: and as ye have heard that antichrist shall come, even now are there many antichrists; whereby we know that it is the last time.

1 John 2:18 · KJV

Already in the apostle John's day there were "many antichrists." Throughout church history, many rulers and public figures have been named as the Antichrist, and each time the naming missed the mark. The spirit that opposes God is at work in every age, so we do not conclude that someone is that final person just because such a figure appears. Scripture gives specific signs by which the final man of sin will be recognized.

  • A covenant — he will confirm a covenant with many for one week (Dan. 9:27).
  • Stopping the sacrifice — in the middle of that week he will cause the sacrifice and the offering to cease (Dan. 9:27).
  • Claiming to be God — he will sit in the temple of God and present himself as God (2 Thess. 2:4; Matt. 24:15).
  • Worship and the mark — he will make people worship the image of the beast, and no one may buy or sell except those who have the mark (Rev. 13:15–17).

We do not look at one political leader, one technology or one system and say, "He has come." We look at whether these signs appear together, as the Word says.

Second: In the pretribulation view, he is revealed after the church has left

In this view, the man of sin appears at the time when the restrainer is taken out of the way (2 Thess. 2:7–8; see ⑩ above). So to conclude that "the Antichrist has already come" while the church is still on earth actually does not fit this view. What we see now is most naturally read not as that final man himself, but as the work of "the mystery of iniquity" that prepares the way for him.

Third: Scripture does not say that the rapture and the start of the seven years fall on the same day

What marks the start of the final seven years is not the rapture but the confirming of a covenant (Dan. 9:27). The rapture may happen at any time before that, but Scripture does not tell us how much time lies between the two. So if we hear news that holds out peace and safety, there is no reason to rush to the conclusion, "There was no rapture, so the pretribulation rapture was wrong." Scripture says that the time when such words are heard is all the more a time to stay awake.

For when they shall say, Peace and safety; then sudden destruction cometh upon them, as travail upon a woman with child; and they shall not escape.

1 Thessalonians 5:3 · KJV

Therefore let us not sleep, as do others; but let us watch and be sober.

1 Thessalonians 5:6 · KJV

Fourth: Five things that will not be shaken, whatever comes first

  • Salvation does not depend on the timing of the rapture. Our hope is not a timetable but Christ alone (Eph. 2:8; Heb. 13:8).
  • The pretribulation rapture is not the belief that "Christians will not suffer." When persecution comes, it does not mean that we have entered the seven years of wrath, and there is no reason for our faith to collapse (John 16:33; 1 Pet. 4:12).
  • In no case will we worship the beast or receive its mark. That mark comes together with a demand for worship, so those who know the Word can discern it (Rev. 14:9–10).
  • We endure to the end and stay faithful unto death (Matt. 24:13; Rev. 2:10; 12:11).
  • We do not follow false christs or announced dates. We do not follow those who say, "The Lord has already come to such-and-such a place," "Gather over here," or "Give up your property" (Matt. 24:23, 26).

9And the third angel followed them, saying with a loud voice, If any man worship the beast and his image, and receive his mark in his forehead, or in his hand, 10The same shall drink of the wine of the wrath of God, which is poured out without mixture into the cup of his indignation; and he shall be tormented with fire and brimstone in the presence of the holy angels, and in the presence of the Lamb:

Revelation 14:9-10 · KJV

But he that shall endure unto the end, the same shall be saved.

Matthew 24:13 · KJV

Fifth: When you hear such news — six steps

StepWhat to doScripture
1Check it against the Word. Confirm it from the text of Scripture, not from videos, rumors or the news.Acts 17:11
2Do not reach a conclusion alone. Discern it together with the pastor of your church and your fellow believers.1 John 4:1
3Keep up your daily life. Do not abandon your work, studies or family. Staying faithful where you have been placed is what being awake looks like.2 Thess. 2:2; 3:12
4Do not spread fear. Do not pass on unconfirmed videos and posts. Comfort one another and build one another up.1 Thess. 5:11
5Share the gospel. Tell neighbors who are shaken that now is the time to turn to the Lord.2 Pet. 3:9
6Lift up your head and pray.Luke 21:28, 36

These were more noble than those in Thessalonica, in that they received the word with all readiness of mind, and searched the scriptures daily, whether those things were so.

Acts 17:11 · KJV

And when these things begin to come to pass, then look up, and lift up your heads; for your redemption draweth nigh.

Luke 21:28 · KJV

Sixth: If you have already been left behind

If you are reading this after the rapture has already happened, the door is not yet shut. Revelation shows a multitude that no one can number coming out of great tribulation. Turn to Christ now. Do not worship the beast, do not receive its mark, and endure to the end.

And I said unto him, Sir, thou knowest. And he said to me, These are they which came out of great tribulation, and have washed their robes, and made them white in the blood of the Lamb.

Revelation 7:14 · KJV

The pretribulation rapture is not "a plan to avoid suffering" but "the hope of waiting for the Bridegroom." We hold this hope on the basis of the Word. And whatever comes first, we pray that on the day the Lord comes for us, we will be standing as a bride who is awake.

Previous7. Seventeen Common Objections and AnswersNext9. Short Questions and Answers
← Contents

9. Short Questions and Answers

QCan we know the date of the rapture?

But of that day and hour knoweth no man, no, not the angels of heaven, but my Father only.

Matthew 24:36 · KJV

And he said unto them, It is not for you to know the times or the seasons, which the Father hath put in his own power.

Acts 1:7 · KJV

No. Bible Insight opposes every attempt to set dates (see Section 10 below).

QDoes this mean there are two Second Comings?

Which also said, Ye men of Galilee, why stand ye gazing up into heaven? this same Jesus, which is taken up from you into heaven, shall so come in like manner as ye have seen him go into heaven.

Acts 1:11 · KJV

There is one Second Coming, and it takes place in two stages (see Objection 8 above). The coming in the air for His saints and the coming to the earth with His saints together make up one return.

QIf there is still a chance after the rapture, is it all right not to believe now?

(For he saith, I have heard thee in a time accepted, and in the day of salvation have I succoured thee: behold, now is the accepted time; behold, now is the day of salvation.)

2 Corinthians 6:2 · KJV

No. That the door of salvation is not shut after the rapture is news of grace, but it does not mean "I can believe later." Keeping the faith in the world left behind means paying a far greater price, even one's own life (Rev. 13:7; 20:4). The time Scripture speaks of is always today.

QIs "the mark of the beast" a vaccine or a chip in the body?

We do not identify any particular product or technology (vaccines, implanted chips, digital currency, cards and so on) as the mark of the beast. The mark in Revelation 13 is received by worshiping the beast and its image. It is a matter of a decision to abandon the faith (Rev. 14:9). It is not something a person receives without knowing it. For decisions about your health, please talk with medical professionals.

QIsn't talk about the end times a way of pushing faith on people through fear?

Wherefore comfort yourselves together, and edify one another, even as also ye do.

1 Thessalonians 5:11 · KJV

We do not mean to cause fear. After describing the rapture, Paul said, "Wherefore comfort one another with these words" (1 Thess. 4:18), and right after speaking of the last days he said, "comfort yourselves together, and edify one another." This section leads not to despair but to hope.

QHow should we treat churches and believers who hold another view?

We honor them as brothers and sisters waiting for the same Lord. It is enough to open the text together and talk humbly. We do not divide churches or condemn others over this question.

QWhat should we do today?

Watch and pray (Luke 21:36), live a holy life, purifying ourselves as He is pure (1 John 3:3), and share the gospel with neighbors who do not yet know Christ (2 Pet. 3:9). People with hope live today more faithfully.

Previous8. What If a Figure Like the Antichrist Appears Without a Rapture?Next10. The Principles of This Section
← Contents

10. The Principles of This Section

We oppose date-setting

Date-setting is teaching that names a particular day or year for the Second Coming or the rapture, and on account of that day leads people to give up their daily lives and their property. The "days" announced again and again in the last century all failed, and they left deep wounds in many families and churches.24 We clearly oppose the following teachings.

  • Teaching that sets a date and announces the Second Coming or the rapture
  • Teaching that tells people to leave their work, studies or families because of that day
  • Teaching that tells people to give away their property because of that day
  • Teaching that claims "direct revelation" in order to make people follow a particular leader

That ye be not soon shaken in mind, or be troubled, neither by spirit, nor by word, nor by letter as from us, as that the day of Christ is at hand.

2 Thessalonians 2:2 · KJV

This section also does not interpret today's international affairs, wars or any particular nation as the fulfillment of prophecy, and it does not name any real person as the Antichrist.

What unites us

Though our views differ, what we confess together is the same: that Jesus will come again as He promised, that the dead will rise again, and that God will make all things new. And that salvation comes through Christ alone (John 14:6; Acts 4:12). As the old saying of the church puts it: in essentials, unity; in non-essentials, liberty; in all things, charity.25

4There is one body, and one Spirit, even as ye are called in one hope of your calling; 5One Lord, one faith, one baptism,

Ephesians 4:4-5 · KJV

He which testifieth these things saith, Surely I come quickly. Amen. Even so, come, Lord Jesus.

Revelation 22:20 · KJV
Previous9. Short Questions and AnswersNext11. Closing Words
← Contents

11. Closing Words

34And take heed to yourselves, lest at any time your hearts be overcharged with surfeiting, and drunkenness, and cares of this life, and so that day come upon you unawares. 35For as a snare shall it come on all them that dwell on the face of the whole earth. 36Watch ye therefore, and pray always, that ye may be accounted worthy to escape all these things that shall come to pass, and to stand before the Son of man.

Luke 21:34-36 · KJV

After describing the rapture, Paul ends with these words: "Wherefore comfort one another with these words" (1 Thess. 4:18). The purpose of the rapture doctrine is not argument but comfort: the comfort of meeting again the believers who have fallen asleep before us, the comfort of being appointed not to wrath but to salvation, and above all the comfort that "so shall we ever be with the Lord." Our views may differ, but we are waiting for the same Bridegroom.

Looking for that blessed hope, and the glorious appearing of the great God and our Saviour Jesus Christ;

Titus 2:13 · KJV

Until the day He comes again, let us keep watch and walk together. Maranatha.

Theological Notes

  1. W. Bauer, F. W. Danker et al., A Greek-English Lexicon of the New Testament (BDAG), 3rd ed., s.v. "ἁρπάζω." The verb describes a sudden removal by a force that cannot be resisted. ↩
  2. The "seventy weeks" of Daniel 9:24–27 are understood as seventy sevens of years, or 490 years. The sixty-nine weeks end when the Messiah is "cut off" (9:26), and the final week of seven years is taken as a period still in the future. Most premillennialists share this framework. See the chapter on Daniel's seventy weeks in J. D. Pentecost, Things to Come. ↩
  3. The main arguments of each view and their critiques of one another are gathered in one volume in Alan Hultberg, ed., Three Views on the Rapture (2010). Because a scholar of each view writes his own case and responds to the others, it is widely used as a fair comparison. ↩
  4. Imminence does not mean "certainly soon." It means "able to happen at any time, without any other prophesied event needing to occur first." The same expectation appears in 1 Cor. 1:7; Phil. 3:20; James 5:8–9; Rev. 22:20. See R. L. Mayhue, "Why a Pretribulational Rapture?" (2002). ↩
  5. On tēreō ek, J. L. Townsend (1980) and T. R. Edgar (1982) understand it as "keeping outside" the hour of trial, while R. H. Gundry (1973) reads it as "protecting within the trial and bringing out at the end." Townsend takes the same expression in John 17:15 to mean keeping a person outside the evil one's influence, and cites it as a parallel usage for Rev. 3:10. Because the verse is debated, we do not draw a conclusion from this verse alone but read it together with the other evidence. ↩
  6. BDAG, s.v. "ἐκφεύγω": to get out of danger or difficulty; to escape. ↩
  7. The correspondence between the Jewish wedding custom and Christ and the church is described in detail in R. E. Showers, Maranatha: Our Lord, Come! (1995), pp. 164–169. We should also remember that the details of the custom differed by region and period. ↩
  8. The word "church" (ἐκκλησία) occurs nineteen times in Revelation 1–3 and again in 22:16. It is not used in the earthly scenes of chapters 4–19. Because this is an argument from silence, it is offered only as a supporting observation, not as independent proof. ↩
  9. For a comparison of the view that the restrainer is the restraining work of the Holy Spirit with the views that it is the Roman Empire, human government or an angel, see the discussion of the restrainer in Pentecost, Things to Come. A minority view reads "falling away" (ἀποστασία) in 2 Thess. 2:3 as a "departure," that is, the rapture itself. This section keeps the usual meaning, "apostasy," and rests the argument on vv. 6–7. ↩
  10. Didache (The Teaching of the Twelve Apostles) 16:1. For the text and a translation, see M. W. Holmes, ed., The Apostolic Fathers, 3rd ed. (2007). ↩
  11. Hippolytus, Commentary on Daniel, book 4. He held that after the sixty-nine weeks, once the gospel had been preached to the whole world, the final week would remain, and that the Antichrist would arise in the middle of that week. This does not mean that he taught a rapture. ↩
  12. The so-called "Pseudo-Ephraem" sermon. It was introduced by T. J. Demy and T. D. Ice, "The Rapture and an Early Medieval Citation" (1995). The interpretation of this sentence is debated among scholars. ↩
  13. Morgan Edwards, Two Academical Exercises on Subjects Bearing the Following Titles: Millennium, Last-Novelties (Philadelphia, 1788). ↩
  14. On the audience and setting of Matthew 24 (v. 15 "the holy place," v. 16 "them which be in Judaea," v. 20 "the sabbath day"), see the relevant chapter of Walvoord, The Rapture Question (rev. ed., 1979). The posttribulational reading is found in Ladd, The Blessed Hope (1956). ↩
  15. This observation also appears in R. L. Mayhue (2002) and in the discussion of 2 Thessalonians 2 in Pentecost, Things to Come. For the opposing view, see Moo's chapter in Hultberg, ed., Three Views on the Rapture (2010). ↩
  16. M. R. Cosby, "Hellenistic Formal Receptions and Paul's Use of ΑΠΑΝΤΗΣΙΣ in 1 Thessalonians 4:17," Bulletin for Biblical Research 4 (1994), argued that the word is not a technical term for a formal reception. R. H. Gundry replied in BBR 6 (1996). We note that both sides belong to an ongoing scholarly discussion. ↩
  17. BDAG, s.v. "παραλαμβάνω" (to take along, to receive) and "αἴρω" (to lift up and remove, to sweep away). Matt. 24:40–41 and John 14:3 use the same verb, παραλαμβάνω. ↩
  18. The three stages of the first resurrection (Christ → the church at the rapture → the Tribulation martyrs) are explained with a chart in the Bible Insight lecture "Three Distinctions in the First Resurrection" (Korean). ↩
  19. Manuel Lacunza, La venida del Mesías en gloria y majestad (completed around 1790, published in the 1810s). In September 1824 the Vatican placed it on the Index of Forbidden Books. English edition: The Coming of Messiah in Glory and Majesty, trans. Edward Irving (London, 1827). ↩
  20. Catechism of the Catholic Church §676 rejects "millenarianism," the idea that the messianic kingdom will be realized within history. ↩
  21. The claim that Darby borrowed the idea from Irving's circle has been made since S. P. Tregelles (1864). Studies refuting it include T. D. Ice, "Why the Doctrine of the Pretribulational Rapture Did Not Begin with Margaret Macdonald" (1990). Both sides generally agree that Irving's own eschatology was closer to historicism. ↩
  22. T. D. Ice (1990); see the note above. Even among posttribulational scholars, who hold the opposing view, there are some who do not accept this "vision-origin" theory. ↩
  23. The Greek τριάς ("triad") first appears in Theophilus of Antioch, To Autolycus 2.15 (about AD 180), and the Latin trinitas first appears in Tertullian (early third century). ↩
  24. In a 2010 survey by the Pew Research Center, 41 percent of Americans said Christ will return to earth by 2050. Belief in the Second Coming is widespread, and for that reason date-setting claims do great harm to the church when they arise. ↩
  25. "In essentials, unity; in non-essentials, liberty; in all things, charity" (In necessariis unitas, in dubiis libertas, in omnibus caritas) is a maxim said to have become widely known through the writings of Rupertus Meldenius, a seventeenth-century German Lutheran theologian. ↩

SECCIÓN ESPECIAL · ESCATOLOGÍA

Arrebatamiento y Segunda Venida: preguntas y respuestasEl arrebatamiento pretribulacional: ¿qué dice la Biblia?

Esta sección no se escribió para calcular fechas ni para causar miedo. Se escribió para comprobar con el texto bíblico la «esperanza bienaventurada» que la Biblia promete, y para ayudarnos a vivir hoy velando. Bible Insight enseña el arrebatamiento pretribulacional como la postura que mejor se ajusta al texto. Al mismo tiempo, respetamos a los creyentes que sostienen otras posturas como hermanos y hermanas que esperan al mismo Señor.

Escrito en noviembre de 2026 · Equipo editorial de Bible Insight
